I just feel incredibly guilty that I've let everything slide.\n\nDoctor: Are these problems still mainly happening because the business collapse and its consequences are ongoing, or did they continue even after things started to improve? Have there been any periods in the last five months when you felt mostly back to normal?\n\nPatient: It has been continuous for the whole five months since the business fell apart, with no breaks or times where I've felt back to normal. The problems are still completely tied to the ongoing mess of losing my work and the financial strain. I really feel like if I could just get the business side sorted out, this whole spiral would finally ease up.\n\nDoctor: Did the collapse of your business involve any life-threatening event, serious injury, or actual violence to you or someone else? Or was it a very stressful loss without that kind of trauma?\n\nPatient: No, there wasn't any violence or life-threatening trauma involved at all. It was just a very stressful financial and professional loss, starting with losing my handyman business, having a dispute with a client, and my chronic back pain worsening around the same time.\n\nDoctor: Around the time your business collapsed, was there any other major stressor or loss—such as a death, separation, legal problem, or worsening health—that also played a big role in how you’ve been feeling? Or does it feel mainly explained by the business collapse itself?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, my chronic back pain worsened significantly right around the same time the business collapsed. It made it impossible for me to do any heavy labor, which pushed me completely out of the workforce and cut off our income. That added a huge amount of financial pressure on my family and really contributed to me shutting down like this.

I just feel incredibly guilty that I've let everything slide.\n\nDoctor: Are these problems still mainly happening because the business collapse and its consequences are ongoing, or did they continue even after things started to improve? Have there been any periods in the last five months when you felt mostly back to normal?\n\nPatient: It has been continuous for the whole five months since the business fell apart, with no breaks or times where I've felt back to normal. The problems are still completely tied to the ongoing mess of losing my work and the financial strain. I really feel like if I could just get the business side sorted out, this whole spiral would finally ease up.\n\nDoctor: Did the collapse of your business involve any life-threatening event, serious injury, or actual violence to you or someone else? Or was it a very stressful loss without that kind of trauma?\n\nPatient: No, there wasn't any violence or life-threatening trauma involved at all. It was just a very stressful financial and professional loss, starting with losing my handyman business, having a dispute with a client, and my chronic back pain worsening around the same time.\n\nDoctor: Around the time your business collapsed, was there any other major stressor or loss—such as a death, separation, legal problem, or worsening health—that also played a big role in how you’ve been feeling? Or does it feel mainly explained by the business collapse itself?\n\nPatient: Yes, my chronic back pain worsened significantly right around the same time the business collapsed. It made it impossible for me to do any heavy labor, which pushed me completely out of the workforce and cut off our income. That added a huge amount of financial pressure on my family and really contributed to me shutting down like this.\n\nDoctor: Compared with what most people might feel after losing work and dealing with worse back pain, does your reaction feel much stronger than expected for the situation? Or does it feel about in proportion to what happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely feels a lot stronger and more extreme than what most people would go through. While losing the business and dealing with my back is hard, freezing up and hiding in the garage or running off to the river over a simple voicemail or paperwork feels like a massive overreaction I can't control. I know I'm just making things worse by shutting down like this."
  }

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I feel like I’ve just completely shut down and keep reacting badly ever since my work fell apart. I can't seem to handle anything that reminds me of trying to get back into business, and it's just... really hard to face.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start soon after your work situation fell apart, or have you been feeling down or worried most days even apart from that? About how long has this been going on?\n\nPatient: It all started within a few weeks of my handyman business collapsing, which was about five months ago. These feelings are definitely tied directly to the work falling apart, because before that I managed things just fine. Have you been able to keep up with your usual responsibilities, or has it significantly interfered?\n\nPatient: It's completely disrupted my life; I've stopped taking any new jobs, let my old customers drift away, and even pulled back from my club and volunteer activities. It's put a massive strain on my marriage because we're short on money, and my wife is so stressed because I'm not pulling my weight at home. I just feel incredibly guilty that I've let everything slide.\n\nDoctor: Are these problems still mainly happening because the business collapse and its consequences are ongoing, or did they continue even after things started to improve? I really feel like if I could just get the business side sorted out, this whole spiral would finally ease up.\n\nDoctor: Did the collapse of your business involve any life-threatening event, serious injury, or actual violence to you or someone else? Or was it a very stressful loss without that kind of trauma?\n\nPatient: No, there wasn't any violence or life-threatening trauma involved at all. It was just a very stressful financial and professional loss, starting with losing my handyman business, having a dispute with a client, and my chronic back pain worsening around the same time.\n\nDoctor: Around the time your business collapsed, was there any other major stressor or loss—such as a death, separation, legal problem, or worsening health—that also played a big role in how you’ve been feeling? That added a huge amount of financial pressure on my family and really contributed to me shutting down like this.\n\nDoctor: Compared with what most people might feel after losing work and dealing with worse back pain, does your reaction feel much stronger than expected for the situation? Or does it feel about in proportion to what happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely feels a lot stronger and more extreme than what most people would go through. While losing the business and dealing with my back is hard, freezing up and hiding in the garage or running off to the river over a simple voicemail or paperwork feels like a massive overreaction I can't control. I know I'm just making things worse by shutting down like this.\n\nDoctor: When you get a voicemail or see paperwork about work, what happens in your body and mind in that moment—do you mainly feel sudden panic symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath, or more like you go numb, freeze, or mentally shut down? About how long do those episodes usually last?\n\nPatient: I don't really experience sudden physical panic like a racing heart; instead, my mind goes completely blank and I'm filled with dread and shame. I just freeze up and avoid the situation entirely by hiding out in my garage or heading down to the river. I'm not sure exactly how long those moments last, but that shut-down feeling generally persists until I can physically get away from whatever triggered it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of reminders about your business and finances, have you been feeling down most of the day or lost interest in almost everything? Or do you feel more like yourself when those stressors aren’t in front of you?"

About how long has this been going on?\n\nPatient: It all started within a few weeks of my handyman business collapsing, which was about five months ago. These feelings are definitely tied directly to the work falling apart, because before that I managed things just fine. I really think if I could get the business side sorted out again, this whole spiral would ease up.\n\nDoctor: Since your business collapsed, how much has this been affecting your day-to-day life, like work, relationships, sleep, or taking care of yourself? Have you been able to keep up with your usual responsibilities, or has it significantly interfered?\n\nPatient: It's completely disrupted my life; I've stopped taking any new jobs, let my old customers drift away, and even pulled back from my club and volunteer activities. It's put a massive strain on my marriage because we're short on money, and my wife is so stressed because I'm not pulling my weight at home. I just feel incredibly guilty that I've let everything slide.\n\nDoctor: Are these problems still mainly happening because the business collapse and its consequences are ongoing, or did they continue even after things started to improve? Have there been any periods in the last five months when you felt mostly back to normal?\n\nPatient: It has been continuous for the whole five months since the business fell apart, with no breaks or times where I've felt back to normal. The problems are still completely tied to the ongoing mess of losing my work and the financial strain. I really feel like if I could just get the business side sorted out, this whole spiral would finally ease up.\n\nDoctor: Did the collapse of your business involve any life-threatening event, serious injury, or actual violence to you or someone else? Or was it a very stressful loss without that kind of trauma?\n\nPatient: No, there wasn't any violence or life-threatening trauma involved at all. It was just a very stressful financial and professional loss, starting with losing my handyman business, having a dispute with a client, and my chronic back pain worsening around the same time.\n\nDoctor: Around the time your business collapsed, was there any other major stressor or loss—such as a death, separation, legal problem, or worsening health—that also played a big role in how you’ve been feeling? Or does it feel mainly explained by the business collapse itself?\n\nPatient: Yes, my chronic back pain worsened significantly right around the same time the business collapsed. It made it impossible for me to do any heavy labor, which pushed me completely out of the workforce and cut off our income. That added a huge amount of financial pressure on my family and really contributed to me shutting down like this.\n\nDoctor: Compared with what most people might feel after losing work and dealing with worse back pain, does your reaction feel much stronger than expected for the situation? Or does it feel about in proportion to what happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely feels a lot stronger and more extreme than what most people would go through. While losing the business and dealing with my back is hard, freezing up and hiding in the garage or running off to the river over a simple voicemail or paperwork feels like a massive overreaction I can't control. I know I'm just making things worse by shutting down like this.\n\nDoctor: When you get a voicemail or see paperwork about work, what happens in your body and mind in that moment—do you mainly feel sudden panic symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath, or more like you go numb, freeze, or mentally shut down? About how long do those episodes usually last?\n\nPatient: I don't really experience sudden physical panic like a racing heart; instead, my mind goes completely blank and I'm filled with dread and shame. I just freeze up and avoid the situation entirely by hiding out in my garage or heading down to the river. I'm not sure exactly how long those moments last, but that shut-down feeling generally persists until I can physically get away from whatever triggered it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of reminders about your business and finances, have you been feeling down most of the day or lost interest in almost everything? Or do you feel more like yourself when those stressors aren’t in front of you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I'm depressed about everything in life in a general way, as these feelings are tied directly to the work and financial stress. When I can escape to the river or my workshop, I actually feel a bit more like myself, though the guilt about avoiding things still lingers. I really believe that if I could just get the business side sorted out, this whole spiral would ease up.\n\nDoctor: When you run off to the river or shut yourself away, have you ever had thoughts of wanting to die or of hurting yourself? Have you made any plans or done anything to act on those thoughts?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I’ve never had any thoughts of hurting myself or wanting to die. Going to the river or hiding in the garage is just a way for me to escape the pressure and stress of the situation. I've never made any plans or done anything to act on thoughts like that.

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
